She thinks of the repulsed grimace her mom would make if she saw her biting her nails, chiding her with something like That’s such a disgusting habit to have, Kate, even as her eyes remain exasperated but soft.

    The image only spurs her anxiety more.

    Three failed tries are what she deems sufficient to give up, at least until she gets back to her apartment, so she throws her phone into on copilot seat and starts the engine.

    Of course, she doesn’t manage to reach home before her phone buzzes next to her, and Kate knows she could wait the roughly ten minutes it will take her to make it back and park, but the trepidation gnawing at her gut forces her to pull into an empty spot and pick up the call on its fifth ring, “Mom.”

    “Kate,” greets her on the other end, and the apology she had ready dies on the tip of her tongue as her puzzlement grows because that voice -

    “Jack?” she tries again, unsure as to why would her step-father be calling her from her mother’s phone, “Where’s mom? She’s been calling me like crazy and I haven’t got the chance to get back to her yet. Is she around? Can you get her?” She didn’t mean to sound so desperate, but when a sigh echoes in her ear and a still silence draws out, the brunette thinks perhaps she wasn’t clear enough.

    Clearing her throat obnoxiously, she calls out her step-father’s name again, takes the phone off her ear to check the call is still on, asks for her mother again. And then Jack speaks, “Honey, where are you right now? Are you home?”

    There’s barely anyone out on a Sunday at ten in the morning, the street outside her window practically deserted save for a couple walking their dog and a bunch of runners trotting up and down the path. “Yes, yes. I’m home,” outright lie, “What’s going on, Jack? Where’s mom?” she repeats, anger clinging to her voice now. If there’s something she can’t stand is to have people beat around the bush with her, for them to treat her like a child who’s not capable of handling things.

    Her instinct tells her this is exactly what’s happening here and she’s not having it.

    “Okay sweetheart, hear me out. Are you by yourself? Is there any chance someone’s with you?” and that’s enough to make Kate bristle and snap, “Jack, spill it out already. Where’s she?”

    Later she’ll admit Jack was right. She should have probably waited to be home to receive the news. She should have called a friend over or gone back to Carol’s as to not be alone. But she’s never been known for her excellent patience or for her ability to think before acting, so when her step-father informs her that her mother was admitted into the hospital two nights ago Kate’s sitting in her car all alone.

    “She didn’t want to worry you, honey. It hasn’t been until recently that they found what was wrong with her, and it has been a long journey of tests and specialists and medication… She didn’t want you to get thrown into it. Eleanor wanted you to stay out there and finish your studies and enjoy your friends, so we thought if things stayed stable then there was no need -“

    Mind still trying to process the first piece of information, still fighting with the idea that her mother was lying on a hospital bed somewhere, a sudden idea dawns on her, “All that stuff about the lawyers, about the company having some kind of legal trouble - that was all bullshit, wasn’t it? It was all a cover.” She’s speaking the thought into existence, her blood running cold when she realizes she doesn’t need confirmation from Jack, the younger girl knows her mother well enough to make sense of it all now.

    Why Eleanor had refused to elaborate about what the issue was, why she never asked Kate to tag along to the meetings when she’s always been so adamant about her getting involved in the family business, why there had been important appointments even during Thanksgivings and Christmas.

    Hell, it was a wonder she hadn’t realized up until now.

    The device on her hand vibrates between her fingers, chimes with a notification she can’t bring herself to check as Jack’s voice forces her back to the present, “Sweetheart, I understand if you’re upset. I tried to reason with your mother but you know how stubborn she can be,” he exhales loudly, and Kate becomes aware for the first time since they began talking of how exhausted he sounds, “She didn’t want me to call you just yet, either, claims she’ll be out in no time. But I think we’ve done enough of hiding things from each other so you should come over whenever you can, honey. I can arrange a plane ticket for you -“

    “No, no. I’ll be on my way right now. I’ll see you soon,” she mumbles, hands shaking as she ends the call and lowers her phone slowly to her lap. She doesn’t wait for Jack to tell her it’s a terrible idea, doesn’t want to hear him say she should wait until she calms down before throwing herself into the highway, so she firmly refuses to pick up the call that brings her phone back to life.

    Kate’s had enough of being told what to do, of people deciding what’s best for her.

    Not even bothering to drop by her house first to pack a bag, Kate finds herself on the road, music blasting loud enough to drown her own thoughts. She’s thankful for knowing this route good enough to be able to drive by pure muscle memory, her eyes focused but her mind hazy and far away.

    Her phone keeps ringing insistently next to her and the brunette is suddenly hit by all the previous times she refused to pick up the call when her mom (or Jack, it seems) stubbornly tried to contact her.

    Jack had said Eleanor wasn’t in immediate danger, but was even it true? And what if she had been? What if her mother had been on the brink of death, trying to reach her, and she had declined the call only because she was busy strolling around or out drinking with her friends? Or worse, what if her mom had died and she hadn’t known about it until two whole days later? Oh god, what if she had missed the chance to hold her mother’s hand one last time?

    What if she hadn’t made it to her fucking funeral?

    I wouldn’t have been able to say goodbye to her just like I couldn’t with dad, and this time it would all have been on me.

    The telltale of panic climbing up her throat and crushing at her chest begins to make itself known. Filling her lungs with oxygen grows to be painful, her breaths coming out short and ragged as she tries to get her hands to stop shaking and her eyes to blink away the tears that have begun to gather there.

    Out of the corner of her eye she sees an exit sign that leads to a small gas station, her brain automatically hitting the blinker as she switches lanes and takes the gravy pathway down to the pitstop.

    Fingers gripping the steering wheel until her knuckles turn white and her nails dig at her own palms, she wills her body to cooperate. Wouldn’t it be just plain embarrassing to end up dead in her car because she had forgotten how to breathe? She’s trying, really, but her mind seems to have erased any instruction as to how to get her lungs to expand and contract on their own.

    What her mind does seem to recall though, is the way Yelena breathes. How she’s taken to counting each inhale and exhale as a way to ground herself, to keep track of the older girl’s feelings, to know if she’s near or if she’s drifting away. And before she’s even aware of it, Kate’s holding her cellphone in her trembling hands, furiously squinting her eyes as she searches through her contacts and purposely ignoring the piling notifications of texts and missed calls.

    Even through her blurry gaze, she discerns the name on her screen, her thumb clumsily hitting the dial icon as she lets her head tilt to the side. Her temple collides with the window, the coolness of the glass a welcome sensation against her burning skin.

    It only takes a single ring this time, “Kate Bishop, care to tell me where the hell are you right now? I went over to your apartment and you weren’t there, on a Sunday morning! I had to ask Pietro of all people, and he didn’t know either, but then he asked Carol and she said you had left her house almost three hours ago. I’ve been worried sick, looking for -“

    “Yel,” she manages to croak out, a pitiful gasp that makes her notice her dry mouth and her hoarse voice. She feels as if she had spent the last two hours screaming. The worst thing is that she can’t, for the life of her, remember if perhaps she had, her whole trip up to here a blur of dodging cars and turning on the correct lanes.

    The other end falls quiet for a second, and Kate doesn’t even need to close her eyes to picture Yelena’s face. She’s probably clutching her phone closer to her ear, straining to listen to even the slightest breath on the brunette’s side. Her mouth must be drawn into a tight line, her bottom lip worried between her teeth, and the two lines that etch themselves in between her eyebrows whenever she’s concerned are definitely making an appearance. Her free hand is either toying with her rings or raking through her hair, and even if Kate doesn’t need to see her to know all of this she wishes she fucking could.

    “Kate,” Yelena whispers cautiously, the sound of her own name rolling out of the blonde’s tongue an even more soothing feeling than the chill of her window, “Kate, where are you?

    Tell me and I’ll go get you.” She’s heard Yelena plead before, but the pure fear in her tone mixes with the relief of simply hearing her voice, a dam breaking then as a sob curses through her body and reverberates across her car and through the phone line.

    This only manages to severe the urgency in Yelena’s voice, “Kate, milaya, can you answer me, please? Where are you? Tell me.” The younger girl knows Yelena is fidgeting restlessly around, the sound of nails clicking against each other and a soft tap tap tap of heels against the floor echo in her ear.

    “My mom,” she cries out, wheezing and terrified. It’s as far as her lungs will let her go, “I can’t -“

    “Kate. Pay attention, malyshka, do you hear me? Listen to me, can you hear me breathing?”

    the Russian interrupts her, forcing Kate to focus back on the calmness of her tone. As she tunes into the sound, she realizes she can actually hear Yelena breathing in and out through the line, the other girl most likely doing an effort to make the action noticeable. “Count them up with me, Kate. Count them and copy me, baby,” Yelena coaxes gently, her voice barely above a whisper. A door opens and closes on the blonde’s end, and suddenly the faint noise that surrounded Yelena disappears and the only thing that’s left are her deep, steady breaths that she encourages Kate to follow.

    Maybe she should be embarrassed about the older girl knowing of her slightly weird habit, but she’s more concerned about dying from oxygen deprivation so she centers her attention on the girl that’s on the phone with her and wills herself to try.

    A couple of frustrated whines fall past her lips at her first failed attempts, but then Yelena actually starts numbering each draw of air as she inhales, and Kate can’t help but cry in relief when dread begins to melt away from her lungs and she feels herself following the pattern.

    “Five, six, seven - that’s it, Kate. Keep going, count them with me, milaya,” and Kate obeys. At first she only mouths the numbers, moving her lips and curling her tongue around the words, but by the twelfth breath she’s recovered her ability to speak. The simplicity of the pattern settles her nervous energy and she relaxes slowly, lets her body grow calm as the pressure behind her eyes dissipates and she greedily fills her lungs with each inhale.

    Yelena doesn’t stop until they reach fifty, letting Kate handle the counting but never skipping an exhale, never breaking the synchronized rhythm they have fallen into. Only when she can clearly hear the younger girl’s steady breathing does she falter, “Kate,” the Russian mumbles carefully, unsure of how much she should press after what had clearly been a panic attack.

    Letting her forehead rest against the wheel, Kate sighs, “My mom’s in the hospital. She’s sick, Yel. I don’t know how bad it is but -“ she cuts herself, clinging tightly to the necklace hanging from her neck and pushing back the tears that still threaten to spill.

    “Okay, okay. Then tell me where you are and I’ll pick you up. We can go there together,”

    Yelena offers immediately, poorly concealed worry coloring her voice. Kate’s still so out of it that she doesn’t even think to ask her where she had been, why she had given her the silent treatment since they came back only to go searching for her without a call first.

    Instead, before Yelena has a chance to worry about her going astray again, the brunette hurries to clarify, “No, no. I’m - I’m close to the city, I swear. Maybe twenty minutes away, half an hour tops.” She’s not exactly sure where she is now, but as she looks down at the clock on her dashboard she knows she’s been out for more than two hours - counting this small hiccup -, so she can’t be much further than that.

    She can feel Yelena’s uneasiness at the situation, so she pushes herself to keep talking,” I just need you to stay on the line with me until I can fully feel my lungs again.” She chuckles demurely, aiming to ease the concern that’s clearly eating away at the older girl.

    What she doesn’t expect - although maybe, in retrospect, she totally should have - is for Yelena to swiftly reply, “I’ll stay with you the whole drive there,” and mean it.

    When Kate feels like her hands have steadied enough (and after Yelena quite literally forces her to head over to the restroom to freshen up and splash some water on her face), the brunette links the call to the speaker of her car and heads for the highway again.

    A husky, deep voice fills the emptiness of her car then, the Russian’s chattering never once wavering. She doesn’t ask for details about Eleanor’s situation, doesn’t offer an explanation for her absence, and doesn’t chide Kate for taking on the road alone (even though Kate knows that will come somewhere later in the near future). The older girl stays on the safer topics, reminiscing about their trip, gossiping about Natasha and Wanda, and asking about Lucky.

    “Oh, god. Lucky!” Kate screeches, suddenly remembering about her furbaby alone in her apartment. This is what happens when you act on a whim, Bishop. Well done, she scolds herself. Yelena doesn’t let her beat herself too much over it though, quickly reminding her that Pietro still has her apartment keys from when they went to Washington and he was left on babysitting duty, “I’ll go grab the keys and take Lucky home with me, don’t worry about it.” And just like that, it’s settled.

    The call stays on while Kate enters the city and while she parks in her mother’s garage. It even stays ten minutes after she kills the engine, the older girl’s voice grounding her as she prepares herself to leave the safety of her car and go face whatever truth awaits her upstairs.

    “I should get going,” the brunette admits ruefully, her eyes constantly flickering towards the screen of her phone as she watches the minutes blink by.

    There’s a heavy sigh at the other end, and then, “Yes, you should. But Kate, please -“ Yelena starts and halts, seemingly looking for the right choice of words, “Please, call me later. Tonight. Let me know how things go, will you?” and she speaks so earnestly, so timidly, that even if Kate had the capability to be mad right now she knows it would all melt away instantly.

    Nodding even though Yelena can’t see her, the younger girl smiles and whispers, “I will. I mean, I called you right now, didn’t I?” It’s not a reproach at all, it’s a fact. In the midst of a terrible mental breakdown, her mind had only conjured a person to reach out to.

    Yelena.

    “You did,” the blonde answers back, the heaviness of the situation finally weighing down on both of them. “Go see your mom, malyshka. Give her a kiss for me,” she mumbles affectionately, and a different kind of tears spring on blue eyes at that.

    Kate switches off the speaker and pulls her phone to her ear, closing the door of her car softly behind her as she licks her lips and wills her voice to stay even, “I will. And I’ll call you later, Yel.”

    “I know. I’ll be waiting.”

    A promise.

Her arms drape around his waist tiredly, her hands clinging to her own wrists to keep them there, and her cheek pushes against his chest as she struggles to follow what she’s sure is a lecture about the perils of driving in a state of panic and the necessity to think before acting on a whim.

    The rumble of his voice dissolve the remaining bits of the adrenaline that had kept her awake and alert enough to get here, the toll of the day’s rollercoaster of emotions hitting her all at once.

    Although it seems that there’s still some room left for surprises, because to her left she hears, “Hey, Katie Kate,” and she lifts her head so fast she feels the room spin.

    “Clint,” is the only thing she manages to say before she launches herself into his arms, tackling him with so much force they would have tumbled to the floor had he not been ready for the attack. He picks her up, the tips of her shoes barely gracing the floor as he spins her around. Just like he always used to. Like her dad did, too.

    And then the dam breaks free and the tears spill from her eyes with vengeance again.

    Her relationship with her godfather has always been amazing. He had been the one to encourage her to follow in his (their, his and her father’s) shoes and study law. He pushed her to join the archery team, bought her her first-ever bow when she turned eleven and her mother wouldn’t budge about it. He’d stepped into the fatherly role without being asked to, always present, always encouraging. I promised your dad, he would always say. When he had to pick her up from a party at two in the morning so her mom wouldn’t throw a fit, or when he had to make a two-hour drive just to see her compete, or when he had to stay behind after class for hours to make sure she would nail her exams.

    Things had gotten a bit strained after her mother had begun to date Jack. The two men had gotten along just fine, but it had been a painful reminder for both Eleanor and Clint to keep seeing each other like they used to. Her mom couldn’t help but be reminded of her late husband every time his best friend would drop by, and for Clint it couldn’t have been not awkward to step into your almost brother’s home to find another man there.

    They had kept in touch - because they were friends too, her mom and Clint - but his visits had slimmed down, and after she transferred to Yale in her third year, she mostly kept in touch with him through the mandatory monthly call and the sporadic text.

    So to say she was fucking grateful that he had come to make sure her mom was alright, to be there for her, would be a total misunderstanding.

    Kate means to ask about Laura, about the kids. Wants to ask how the gap year has been treating him. Wants to tell him about her new friends, about Yelena. But the only thing that she gets through her lips is a pitiful whimper that has his arms tightening around her.

    Another hand falls on her shoulder, Jack squeezing affectionately before suggesting they move to the living room. Clint pats her back firmly as he detangles her from his embrace, his concerned eyes gazing across her face, “She’s strong, Katie. If there’s someone who can pull through whatever it is, that’s Eleanor. I mean, she could handle your dad. If that’s not impressive then I don’t know what is.” They both wince softly at the mention of Derek Bishop, but it still manages to get a watery chuckle out of the younger girl and a crooked smile from her godfather.

    Then her mind catches on the whatever it is and Kate feels all her pent-up anger at being left in the dark surge right back up. “Jack,” she calls, voice rasp but firm as she strides after her step-father, “You’ve got to tell me,” and it’s not a request, it’s a demand.

    A bottle of water materializes in front of her eyes, Clint nodding at it in a silent command, and as she fumbles with the cap she hears Jack take a deep breath, “You have to understand, Kate. We’ve only known what it was for certain a couple of months ago, not even while you last came down here.”

    “But you did know something was wrong then and you didn’t tell me. Hell, you knew from way back, didn’t you? Thanksgivings? Summer? How long have you guys been keeping this from me?” Kate knows she shouldn’t yell at him, that he had just been following her mom’s wishes. Deep down she can even understand why her mother would think she was protecting her, so she doesn’t even want to yell at her. But there’s so much frustration and resentment at being kept in the dark about something so vital that she doesn’t know how to cope with it.

    Plopping down on one of the loveseats, Kate feels the cushions dip immediately as Clint takes a seat next to her. Jack buys some time by the liquor cabinet before walking to stand in front of them, offering a whisky to Clint and a painfully remorseful smile to Kate.

    “We’ve known something was off since mid-August,” he admits quietly, his free hand rubbing tiredly across his forehead, “She was having headaches almost daily. At first, the doctors wrote it off as migraine. Gave her some painkillers and called it a day. But as time passed she would lose her balance sometimes, she began to have blurry vision for extended periods of time…” and Jack is still standing right in front of her, Kate can see his lips moving, but she’s stopped listening. She feels as if she was underwater, all the noises muffled by some invisible barrier that leaves her alone with her thoughts.

    Suddenly she’s eight again. She can see herself sitting exactly where she is now, except this time it’s her mom the one sitting in front of her, explaining very calmly why her dad has been going so frequently to the doctor’s office, why he sometimes throws up even when he’s not sick to the stomach, why he seems more tired than usual.

    “Cancer,” she hears through the ringing in her ears, and it’s only when she becomes aware of the two pairs of worried eyes on her that she realizes she was the one to utter that cursed word.

    There really is no need for Jack to confirm it, the grim look on his face and the wet shine in his eyes tells her everything she needs to know. It doesn’t stop her mind from spinning though, because it seems impossible that this is happening again. This shouldn’t be happening again.

    Taking a deep breath, she tries to calm down before speaking again, “I’m going to see her.” She doesn’t ask, doesn’t need permission, only for them to tell her where her mother is.

    Out of the corner of her eye she sees Clint stand up too, probably gauging up the situation before he decides to speak. “Kate, maybe you should rest a bit before we head over there?

    You look a bit rough,” his blue eyes dart up and down Kate’s body, stay particularly focused on her hands. It’s then she becomes aware of the trembling of her fingers, stubbornly curling them into fists and willing them to stop.

    “No, I’ve done enough waiting. I’m going now,” she doesn’t mean to snarl it like that, regrets it the second she sees his hands go up in surrender, placating her. She hates to upset people, but it’s in moments like this she realizes she has absolutely no idea how to deal with her own pain.

    Exactly as she had expected Jack moves toward her, a gentle smile on his face. He’s probably going to tell her she should wait a bit, freshen up, sleep, whatever. She doesn’t want to hear it. She’s about to say as much when he beats her to it, “Katie, she won’t admit to it but she’s scared too. If we go see her and you’re still so shaken, she’ll worry even more,” he speaks softly but firm, and it’s probably the lack of patronizing in his voice that gets through her.

    Her step falters and her fists uncurl because she hates to admit it but he’s making sense. “Why don’t you go up to your room, take a quick nap while I get lunch ready, and we go visit Eleanor after,” Clint tries again, earning a skeptical look from her and a snort from Jack. “He means to say while I get lunch ready and he sulks around,” her stepfather amends, and Clint’s offended sputtering is enough to crack a smile out of her.

    Running her hands down her face, Kate sighs and lets her shoulders sag, “Okay, okay. You’re right,” she concedes, words gritted through her teeth. She’s still angry - at what exactly remains unclear - so she bolts up the stairs and barely acknowledges Jack’s “We’ll call you down, Katie,” with a grunt.

    Her bed has never looked as appealing as it does the moment she opens her bedroom door.

    Kate lets herself fall face-first into it, fingers digging at her comforter and burrowing her cheek on her pillow. She could very well fall asleep just like this, but somehow her childhood room now holds a relentless memory of Yelena, of the time they spent there together, and the brunette only needs a minute of wistful reminiscing before she remembers she promised to call the Russian.

    Moving is a hassle but the trouble she’d get in if she didn’t let Yelena know she had made it back home safe would be far worse, so she grumbles the whole time but does stand up and reach for her backpack. “Thank god I had the right mind to go over to Carol’s with my wallet and my phone”, she mumbles to herself as she unlocks her screen.

    Kate debates on whether to call the older girl or send a quick text telling her she’s okay, finally figures she has already taken enough energy from the other girl with her (very much granted) mental breakdown earlier, and so settles for typing already home, will see mom later. i’ll let you know how it goes before hitting send.

    The answer comes so fast Kate has to double-check the time to make sure she didn’t doze off at some point. Either Yelena was already texting someone or she had been waiting with her phone in her hands for Kate’s sign of life.

    Turns out they may have been thinking about writing to the other at the same time, because the text she gets first is not a reply but a cute picture of Lucky sitting at the back of a car the brunette doesn’t recognize. Below the pic she can read He’s very happy with his vacation followed by an unnecessary amount of exclamation marks. By the time she’s done zooming in her dog’s lolling tongue she does get her answer

    Great malyshka, try to rest. See you soon.

    The stupid stutter her heart does when she sees that word will never cease to amaze her, and neither will the stupid grin it pulls out of her stop to embarrass her. Kate had - with the unknowing help of Pietro - managed to discover how that damn word was spelled so she could finally translate it and be sure of its meaning. Sure, she could have asked Yelena directly, but a small part of her had been worried that the blonde may stop using it if she knew Kate was conscious of the definition. Now though, seeing it written so clearly and knowing that Yelena is aware the younger girl could search it if she wanted to, there’s a special giddiness thrumming in her body.

    They still have to talk (a real talk, a what-are-we talk, a why-did-you-go-MIA-again talk) but for now, Kate clings to that happy feeling as she closes her eyes and lets exhaustion take over her body. The rest can wait until she gets back to New Haven.

    Retrospectively though, if she had been more perceptive, if she had paid attention to something other than the cute Russian pet name her friend had taken to use with her, Kate would surely have dwelled a bit deeper at the See you soon. Hell, if she had been on her regular quick and analytical grind, she would have wondered why was Lucky in a car when hers and Yelena’s apartment wasn’t really so far apart.

    But she had been sleeping about five hours a night tops for three days straight, and then had kind of broken up her friends-with-benefits thing while still being on the dark about the current situation of her friends-with-no-benefits-but-lots-of-feelings, and then learned that her mother was in the hospital and so proceeded to drive all the way down to New York. She had even managed to squeeze in a panic attack on her way. So yeah, sue her if she’s not the sharpest she’s ever been.

    That’s absolutely the reason why, exactly two hours and twenty-three minutes later, she wakes up to the sound of voices being rudely loud somewhere on the floor below her room.

    She’s about to turn around and try to drift off again when she hears scratching. Like, actual, honest to god scratching of nails again wood. “What the hell,” Kate mutters as she tilts her head towards the door. The noise persists and so does her stupefaction because it sounds like there’s a fucking rat trying to open her door and she’s had more than enough excitement for today -

    “Lucky!” she yells dumbfounded when her golden retriever finally manages to push through the door and comes trotting eagerly to jump into bed with her. Her hands furrow deep in his fur as Kate hides her face in the crook of his neck while he tries to reach around to lick her cheek, “What are you doing here, boy. You’re supposed to be with -“

    And then her brain finally kicks into action and she springs out of bed and bolts to the stairs, because there’s no fucking way that she -

    “Yelena,” she gasps, her foot hovering unsurely over the last step as she takes in the picture in front of her. Her brain is trying to discern if she’s really awake or if she’s in the midst of a painfully realistic dream when she gets a literal nudge in the right direction, courtesy of her dumbass of a dog who trips himself over the stairs in a rush to join the party and crashes into her legs.

    The commotion brings out Clint from wherever he was hiding, and he steals her chance at a decent greeting to the two women standing next to Jack in her home’s foyer when he practically squeals, “Natasha Romanoff! Is that really you?”

    A mix of confusion and surprise falls over the rest of them as her stepfather and the redhead stare at each other for about two seconds before they both burst into laughter and hurry to greet with a hug.

    Kate finds Yelena’s astounded gaze, green eyes clearly asking What the hell is going on? She imagines her eyes must give off the same stunned vibe when she helplessly shrugs her shoulders in what’s clearly an I have absolutely no fucking idea.

    “I take your sister knows Clint,” Jack whispers to Yelena, and god bless that men’s observation skills. The blonde barely manages to nod as she continues to witness the exchange of shoulder pats and arm punches between the other two.

    At least Jack’s words do seem to break them from their reunion elation, Natasha being the first one to acknowledge them, “I met him as Professor Barton, but yeah I do,” she says grinning up at Clint, “He gave me hell all through my degree, was an absolute pain in my ass.”

    “But it paid off didn’t it? I hear you’ve been causing trouble up there,” the older man interjects, chuckling proudly as Natasha mumbles hell yeah before launching on a tirade of questions about what she’s been up to. Clint drags her to the main living room with an arm draped around Natasha’s shoulders, the two babbling away like they were long-lost best friends and completely forgetting about the other the rest.

    Jack’s gaze follows them as they disappear into the room, his joyful smile evaporating as his eyes widen and he practically yells “The food!” before scurrying away towards the kitchen. And then it’s only them.

    After a beat of silence Kate realizes Yelena has yet to speak, but when the older girl takes a tentative step forward, looking unsure of how to proceed, Kate lunges at her. Thankfully Yelena seems to be as ready for it as the brunette feels because strong arms immediately circle around her waist, tightening and pulling her closer still.

    It all feels so perfect that Kate has to move her head back to gaze at Yelena’s face, bringing her hands to her face to touch her cheeks and prod at her hair to convince herself this is not something her mind has conjured. “I’m so glad you’re here, Yel,” she mumbles in awe, eyes glossy when she realizes that the other girl really is there touching her. That she’s hauled her sister and Kate’s dog into a car to drive all the way here just to be with her.

    “Are you?” the older girl asks, tone dripping with vulnerability as she watches her cautiously, “I worried maybe you wouldn’t. Because I didn’t answer. Yesterday. But Kate I promise there’s a reason -“

    Something inside her chest swells and Kate swallows hard, her fingers digging deeper in Yelena’s blue sweatshirt. She doesn’t need an explanation right now, the confirmation of its existence is more than enough, so she doesn’t wait for the blonde to finish. Instead, she pushes their foreheads together and lets her eyes fall close. “I am,” she mumbles vehemently, the feeling of eyelashes tickling her making her smile for the first time in what feels like forever, “I - God, I am. So much,” and she hadn’t even stopped to think about how much she would actually need her until Yelena was right next to her. Now the idea of facing this alone seems way scarier than it did before, so yes, she’s only spilling the truth.

    Honestly, Kate’s pretty sure she could stay there in the middle of the hall clinging to Yelena and committing her scent to memory, but as things always tend to go a throat clearing behind them forces them to put some distance between them. Jack stands there with an indulgent smile and a plate of something steamy held in between his oven mittens, “Lunch’s ready,” he gives a little shake to the food as his eyes wander to the clock sitting on the wall, “If we get started now we could go see Eleanor when the afternoon visiting hours start.”

    That’s enough to kick them into movement. Yelena follows Jack into the kitchen to help him and gather the cutlery while Kate takes it upon herself to fetch the two newly reunited friends. She finds them already sitting at the table - which, convenient - both huddled together over what seems to be Natasha’s phone as they discuss something in hushed tones.

    “Am I interrupting a possible plot to take over the world?” she asks loudly to make herself known, and even though she knows her voice sounds hoarse and she must look exhausted and depressed as hell, neither comment on it. Natasha breaks into a grin first, shaking her head as she pushes her phone towards Kate, “I was showing Clint a picture of Wanda,” the redhead answers simply, and it’s such a sweet sentiment that it almost brings tears to Kate’s eyes again.

    No one can blame her though, her feelings are all over the place today.

    The picture turns out to be one of Wanda and Yelena, the two wearing a ceremony gown that lets Kate know it was probably taken on their graduation. Something tells her that they were talking about something deeper than just Natasha’s relationship with her friend, the way Clint eyes her curiously giving them away, but she pretends to be none the wiser as she slides on the empty chair next to her godfather and smiles.

    Surprisingly, she’s saved from facing the burning question at the tip of Clint’s tongue when Natasha suddenly stands up and walks around to her chair. Wordlessly, she leans down and envelops Kate in a warm hug. It lasts a total of three seconds (and if anyone says the brunette was counting they’d be liars), the older girl pulling away but keeping her hands on her shoulders. “Sorry, Kate. I let this one distract me from greeting you before,” Natasha snickers as she winks conspirational over her shoulder, but then her girn fades and her eyes soften, “If you ever need anything, mom-related or not, you know where to find me.” And she speaks so seriously Kate can only nod sharply once and stare owlishly up at her.

    Natasha returns to her seat when Jack and Yelena come barreling through the door, the blonde crooking her eyebrow up curiously at her sister’s stance but otherwise choosing to stay quiet. The small tilt at the corner of Yelena’s lips betrays the smile that she’s concealing, but Kate doesn’t get too much time to admire it because once the redhead moves over to help she’s left alone with Clint. He lets out an obnoxious low whistle that catches her attention and immediately makes her narrow her eyes at him.

    It doesn’t deter him though, “Wow, so you’ve really got it bad, huh?” and how the hell could he be saying that with just one quick glance if he hadn’t been gossiping like a fucking teenager with none other than Natasha Romanoff before she walked in. The only thing that makes her falter is the fact that she’s sure Natasha wouldn’t be airing any of these kinds of private business to anyone - friend or otherwise - considering Yelena’s situation, so Kate actually stops to wonder if she’s truly so pathetically into the Russian than even the doorman of her building would be able to tell.

    If we take into consideration that virtually every stranger they’ve encountered has given for a fact that they were a couple well, maybe she is that obvious.

    The infuriatingly smug smirk Clint has on his face doesn’t leave at any point during their lunch. The only reason Kate abstains from outrightly punching him on the shoulder is Yelena’s hand wrapped tightly around her own. She risks her godfather chancing a glance beneath the table and seeing it because honestly, it feels so nice she couldn’t care less about the endless mockery she’d have to endure.

    Plus, she does get to kick him on the shin so that makes them even.

    She’s actually really thankful for Natasha’s presence, almost as much as she is for Yelena’s, because her unexpected friendship history with Clint drives the attention off her and into their countless anecdotes from college and beyond. She knows if it had been only Jack, Clint, and her there would have either been a slightly uncomfortable silence or a bunch of questions about how she’s feeling, so she’ll take any out she can get.

    When it’s finally time to leave Kate feels as if she’s been home for weeks instead of a few hours, both exhausted and restless at the same time. Jack is getting ready to go while Clint assures the brunette that he’s also going even if it’s only to wait outside while she sees Eleanor if that’s what Kate wants. She nods noncommittally and turns to look at the two sisters hanging by the library, watches as Yelena points to some of the books her mom had shown her during Thanksgivings, offering them to her. “Or, you know, she could go with you and I could sit this one out? Stay here with Nat, maybe rope her into helping me get ready some papers your mom asked me to look over for her,” her godfather suggests, mumbling deliberately as he looks over her shoulder following her line of sight. Kate knows he thinks he’s being sleek. She also hopes the dirty look she sends him conveys that he’s absolutely not.

    “Oh, come on. I’m trying to be like - encouraging and supporting and everything. I’m a cool dad,” he says as he rolls his eyes exasperatedly, but by the way he pointedly says he’s a cool dad Kate knows he’s not just referring to being a father to his actual children, and so all her annoyance at his antics melts away as she slaps him lightly on the shoulder with a watery smile on her face.

    Giving her a thumbs up, he actually shoves her forward almost causing her to lose her valance (and what’s with everyone rudely pushing her around today?). Her clumsy entrance manages to catch the Russians’ attention though, Natasha smiling gently at her before walking over to where Clint is already pulling a folder out of his briefcase (and where the hell had he kept that all this time?), effectually leaving her and Yelena alone.

    The older girl circles her fingers around Kate’s wrist and begins to rub her thumb over her pulse point, “Are you ready to leave yet?”

    “Actually, I was wondering if you’d come with,” she speaks hurriedly, her words a jumbled mess, but she’s beginning to get anxious again at the idea of her mother being in an actual hospital, at the realness of her mom being sick with fucking cancer right now as they speak, and Kate’s afraid she may lose her nerve if she doesn’t just blurt it out.

    Something in her must clue Yelena into the rising panic she’s feeling. Maybe it’s the quiver in her voice or maybe it’s the racing heartbeat thrumming beneath her fingers, but whatever it is, it’s enough for the blonde to look her straight in the eyes and nod just as fast. “Yes. Yes, of course. I’ll come with you, Kate Bishop,” Yelena mumbles tenderly, an edge of something the younger girl can’t quite place in her voice but that feels warm and sweet all the same.

    A knock on the library door makes them both turn. “Shall we?” Jack asks carefully, eying Kate with a mild concern he’s not able to disguise. For a second she wonders if her mom is in a bad physical state, too. Like, maybe she’s begun to take some medication, maybe she’s been losing weight, maybe her hair has been thinning out, maybe her skin is getting asheen.

    Deep down she knows it’s unlikely, that’s she’s being irrational, but the not-knowing is spinning her brain out.

    Tugging at her wrist, Yelena is the one that forces her to move. They get their jackets silently and follow Jack to the garage, Kate only realizing she’s completely foregone brushing her hair or fixing her mess of an attempted make-up since she woke up from her improvised nap. She must look absolutely terrible, but as she runs a hand through her brown locks she can’t really find it in herself to care.

    The drive to the hospital feels dragged out in the worst way possible, almost as if she was seeing the whole thing from an outer perspective, an out-of-body experience. Yelena and Jack carry on some mindless conversation over the music that’s playing on the radio station, but neither tries to get Kate to participate.

    Jack offers to go in first, to check with the doctors and to let Eleanor know that Kate’s here to see her. He trails off after a nurse even though he already knows the room her mother is in, and Yelena guides her by the elbow to force her into a chair while they wait.

    It’s obvious that the Russian wants to say something by the way she fidgets with her rings and casts side-eye glances at Kate, but she’s also probably aware that Kate’s been deep inside her mind since they left the penthouse. She settles for linking their fingers together and waiting the brunette out, both girls sitting in silence as people move frantically around them. The soft touch helps ground Kate, pulls her out of her own head and brings her back to the present. Yelena tilts her head to the side, gaze gentle as she smiles encouragingly at the younger girl. Kate squeezes the hand that’s woven into hers.

    When Jack comes to fetch Kate to lead her into the hallway Yelena stands up but lets go of the brunette’s hand. She looks unsure as to whether she’s expected to follow along or stay there, but it only takes a second for Kate to reach around and grasp at her wrist to drag her down the corridor with her.

    Maybe she’ll go inside alone, Kate’s not made up her mind yet, but she’s not letting the other girl out of her sight just yet.

    The three of them make their way down the hallway and stop at room number 47. It’s such an inconspicuous number, so irrelevant it makes Kate irrationally mad. Her mother has been confined to this fucking hospital room because she’s sick and could die and the world seems to move on anyway, to not give a damn about how everything is tilting off its axis for her. She doesn’t notice she’s been digging her nails into her palms until Yelena yelps softly, and then Kate realizes she still has one of her hands curled around the blonde’s skin and that she’s hurt her.

    “Fuck. Shit, Yel. I’m sorry,” she gasps, her hand dropping Yelena’s wrists as if she had been burned, “I didn’t mean to hurt you.” She folds her arms across her chest, hides her hands on the crooks of her elbows, and fists her sweater instead. Her eyes dart across the door uneasily, and she’s still so busy stewing in her unreasonable anger that she barely hears Jack walk off or Yelena tsk next to her. “I know,” the blonde begins softly, completely unconcerned about the red half-moons sitting on her fair skin, “Are you… Do you want me to come in with you?”

    Kate only needs three breaths to consider her answer, “Yes.”

    The room her mother is staying in is considerably big. There’s a huge window that overlooks the left side of a parking lot, three seats that look a bit worn but otherwise not completely uncomfortable, a small table with things scattered on top - both personal stuff and medicines -, a TV hanging up the wall next to a tall wardrobe, and a plain white door that leads to the bathroom.

    And right in the middle, there’s one of those beds that bend up and down, flanked with railings on both sides. Her mom looks way too small there, buried beneath a navy blue bed sheet with a blanket draped over her feet. She’s got an IV on her left arm, palm up on top of a small pillow. She looks exactly like she did the last time Kate saw her, hair clean and down her shoulders, her eyelashes holding some black mascara, her lips tinted pink. Eleanor looks out of place in this hospital, and it’s such a jarring sight it leaves Kate speechless.

    Yelena is hovering behind her, waiting for Kate to move. But when it becomes clear that Kate may need a push the blonde is happy to walk to her side and lay a hand on the small of her back. She doesn’t move it, doesn’t push, but the gesture is enough to make Kate’s feet cooperate.

    Stumbling into the room, the younger girl doesn’t get the chance to cast a grateful look at Yelena before her mom sees her and she speaks, “Kate, honey.” And the relief in her mother’s voice, the worry she hears there does it for her. Kate rushes to her side and throws her arms around Eleanor’s shoulders, buries her face in her mother’s chest. The older woman begins to stroke her hair and rubs a comforting hand up and down her back, and she smells faintly of antiseptic and generic detergent and something so distinctly home that Kate can’t help but remember her dad then. That only serves to bring a new wave of fresh tears to her eyes, the familiarity of the slightly awkward comforting pats her mom leaves between her shoulderblades forcing a sob out of her.

    Kate’s not sure how long she stays there, body uncomfortably bent over her mom’s hospital bed as she cries into her robe. What she does notice is that when she’s finally pulled herself together and remembered that she should be the one doing the comforting, Yelena is nowhere to be seen.

    “She left the room a while ago, waved before closing the door,” Eleanor speaks first for the second time this evening. Her blue eyes follow Kate’s wandering ones, eyebrows drawn together with concern when they land on her daughter’s tear-stained reed cheeks. She curls her hand around Kate’s elbow to catch her attention, points a finger towards the chair on the corner, and motions at her to bring it closer. The brunette moves dazed, rubbing the back of her hand stubbornly across her face to get rid of the dampness on her skin.

    The stern look on her mother’s face is not what she was expecting to find once she sat next to her. “Jack told me you jumped into your car and drove straight here after your trip,” Eleanor chides upset, her overprotectiveness taking over. Kate’s reminded then why exactly she had to learn of her mom’s sickness months after it was diagnosed, her mouth curving in a full scowl. “Yeah well, if you had treated me like an actual adult and talked to me about what landed you here in time, I wouldn’t have had to panic.”

    Maybe reproaching things right now was not the best course of action though, guilt brimming in her gut the moment her mother’s eyes cast downwards. “I’m sorry, mom,” she sighs, bringing a hand to rub tiredly at her temples, “I don’t mean to pick a fight, I’m sorry. But really, this has to stop. You can’t protect me from everything - you’ve got to believe I’m capable of handling things, no matter how hard they may be.” Kate grips her mother’s hand earnestly, “And I’m so, so sorry for not picking up the phone mom, you’ve got -“ her breath catches in her throat as she forces herself not to break down in tears again, “You’ve got no idea how scared I was, when Jack - when he said you where here. I was terrified, and I thought I may have lost you without even knowing it. I don’t want to feel like that ever again.”

    Sadness shines all across Eleanor’s face, fingers curling tightly around Kate’s own hand. “I’m sorry,” the older woman whispers vehemently, “I’m sorry too, darling. I wanted to believe I would be able to solve this by myself, to save you the trouble of going through this again, but I was wrong.”

    Honestly, she was expecting way more of a fight from her mom. Definitely a twenty-minute long lecture about how careless she’d been acting on a whim, probably a bunch of reasons why she’d felt justified to keep this from her. When Eleanor goes straight for the apology Kate takes a closer look at her, notices the dark circles under her eyes, the purple splotches that litter the skin of her arm where they must have been sticking needle after needle, the limpness of her hair, the sickly pale color of her skin. It’s all there, buried underneath a thin layer of makeup and attitude, and the brunette is suddenly hit by the physical tangency of her mom’s very real sickness.

    There are a million questions burning on the tip of her tongue: How bad is it? How’s the treatment going? What’s the treatment exactly? How long does she have to undergo it? Are they going to intervene? Will she be able to return home anytime soon?

    What she says instead is, “It’s okay, mom. We’ll be okay,” because right now her mom needs to hear it, needs Kate to believe it for the two of them, and so she will.

    A soft knock keeps them from saying anything else, both Bishop women turning their heads toward the door. There, Yelena’s head peaks into the room tentatively, “I brought you some water,” the blonde says, Kate suddenly aware of the rawness of her throat.

    It’s up to Eleanor to coax the other girl inside, “Thank you, Yelena. You are a dear.” And then, because her mom is still a mom and she won’t lose the chance to embarrass her, she continues, “I’m so glad to see you again. We were very sad to miss you on Christmas, Kate especially.”

    That draws a bashful I’m happy to see you too from Yelena and a poorly concealed groan from Kate, but it’s comforting in a way. The fact that her mother won’t let her live down her obvious crush on the older girl is a sign that Eleanor may be sick, but she’s in no way out of her wits.

    Her mother takes it upon her to carry the conversation, firing Yelena question after question about the internship her friend had offered the Russian. Kate has half a mind to intervene, to save the other girl from the tireless interrogatory, but the happy glint on Yelena’s eyes lets her know the blonde is actually glad to answer, to share these details with her mom, and so she lets herself melt down on her chair and just listen to the two women go back and forth about the pros and cons of - the New York subway? She’s not exactly sure how they got there, but she will gladly take the distraction. Her mom looks relaxed for the first time since she came into the room, probably thankful to be able to talk about something other than what’s confined her there, and there’s a warm feeling inside Kate’s chest at the sight of her mother and her - person being so comfortable around each other.

    They keep talking until Jack comes to tell them visitors’ hours are almost up, and then he suggests Kate comes with him to get the daily update from the doctor before leaving. Kate’s eyes dart between both her parents, from Jack’s sure stance to Eleanor’s dubious stare, unsure of how to act. She wants to go, wants someone to solve some of her doubts, but she doesn’t want to do it against her mother’s wishes.

    “I can stay here meanwhile, I’ll keep her company,” Yelena peeps up then, a leg drawn up to her chest and a reassuring smile on her lips. It’s aimed at Eleanor, and surprisingly the older woman just nods, “We’ll wait here,” her mother confirms, her words a clear pass for Kate that has her following after Jack out of the room and down the hallway.

    The doctor speaks kindly but without preambles: they’ve located a small tumor at the back of her mother’s brain, the size of a small ping pong ball. They’ve yet to certify if there’s more, and until then there won’t be an intervention. She’s being treated for all her symptoms and they are rather optimistic about the tests confirming there’s nothing else hidden, the corroboration being the last step they need before deciding how to proceed. Chemotherapy is at the top of the doctor’s suggestions, but he’s willing to go through all of their options when the time comes.

    Kate leaves the doctor’s office overwhelmed but curiously reassured. Dr. Jaman had been honest about her mother’s diagnosis, but he had also stated that Eleanor’s chances of a full recovery were definitely on the table, so no matter how long the road may end up being the brunette was going to cling to that hope and wait.

    The three of them leave the hospital once Jack and Kate say goodbye to Eleanor with a promise to drop by tomorrow again. The younger girl chooses not to comment on the slightly glazed-over eyes both Yelena and her mother bear, the two of them huddled close together whispering when they had entered the room again. Whatever it was the blonde and her mother were discussing, she was happy to let them keep it to themselves for now.

    This time the drive back to the penthouse feels lighter. She’s seen her mom, held her and kissed her and apologized and understood the full situation, so Kate even indulges in Jack’s corny jokes and Yelena’s excitement about the city. All her energy leaves her the second they cross the main door though, her knees almost giving up underneath her from the exhaustion of the day. The older girl seems to catch her change of demeanor, an arm quickly curling around her waist as she eyes her apprehensively, “Are you feeling okay?”

    Jack is already by the living room where Natasha and Clint were waiting, probably debriefing her godfather about the latest updates, and so Kate lets herself sag against Yelena’s body without the fear of both men hovering around her worriedly. “Yeah, just tired, y’know?” she practically speaks on Yelena’s hair, her face resting on the Russian’s temple. Kate feels the older girl relax and nod, and then she suggests they move up to the couch so the brunette can rest.

    “Actually let’s go up to my room,” Kate says, her worn-out brain preventing her from feeling any form of self-consciousness at her words, “We need to talk.” She knows she would be way more nervous about the incoming conversation was she any less exhausted, but she’s pretty sure she’s reached her limit of emotions for the day. Besides, Yelena is here, climbing the stairs next to her and willing to talk. That has to mean something, right?

    Unlike Kate, Yelena does seem anxious. The Russian closes the door behind them and watches silently as Kate walks toward her bed and plops down on top of the pillows, one knee drawn up to her chest as both her arms circle her leg. Yelena stays rooted on the spot, fingers linked together to keep them from fidgeting with her rings. The brunette has to stifle a smile at the very obvious attempt, mainly because instead of her rings Yelena begins to clink her thumbnails together in a distinct rhythm. And even if that wasn’t telling enough, the way she keeps shifting her weight discreetly from one foot to the other or the subtle twitch on the left corner of her mouth would be.

    Before Kate gets the chance to dwell too much on how - or when - she’s grown so expert on reading the other girl - or wonder if the opposite applies, too - Yelena breaks the silence. “You wanted to talk?” she says after clearing her throat, leaning her back on the doorframe.

    “Actually, it was more of an invitation for you to talk, babe,” Kate breathes quietly, eyebrows furrowing together as she recalls the turmoil she had felt the previous day at the blonde’s clear avoidance, “Maybe explain why you disappeared on me after we came back?” The again goes unsaid but rings loudly in the space between them anyway.

    Still, Kate doesn’t want this to begin as a confrontation, has had enough of that for a lifetime already, so she pats the empty space on the bed next to her and sends what she hopes feels like a warm, encouraging smile at the older girl. Yelena moves wordlessly, lets her body fall onto the mattress, and closes her eyes.

    For the first time today, Kate takes a good, close-up look at her. There are dark circles underneath her eyes, redness around the rims when green orbs reveal themselves to her. She looks rough, like she hasn’t slept in a while. Then the brunette’s eyes drift down to Yelena’s hands, still tightly clutched together, and notices the short, blunt nails that have been bitten almost to the root. Concern overcomes her slight upsetness as Kate takes Yelena’s fingers with her own, bringing them closer to her face to inspect the tiny wounds that surround her cuticles. She’s so preoccupied with the blonde’s hands that she almost misses her words.

    “I told them,” Yelena speaks so quietly, her voice barely above a whisper, that at first Kate thinks she’s imagined it. Her head snaps to the right instantly, her wide eyes already looking at her. The older girl tightens her hand around Kate’s, her lips curling downwards as her bottom lip wobbles. She’s holding back tears, Kate realizes, and then, after she takes everything in, her mind goes over what Yelena had just said.

    I told them.

    Thankfully she’s quick enough to avoid being obnoxious and asking who’s them. Honestly, it only takes a glance at the glassiness of Yelena’s eyes and her poorly concealed trembling mouth. What takes longer to sink in though is the reality of what Yelena has just said, the what she had told them.

    It gives Kate pause, makes her doubt herself and her train of thought for a minute. But then, “They called on Saturday, wanted to see how the trip had been. They asked me about you -

    because they know about you, they know we’re friends and they were glad to know I had a close friend. But mama wouldn’t stop going on and on about how important it was that I had a female friend that was so close, and how beautiful platonic, friendly relationships are, and I just - I felt like she knew, like she was trying to subtly remind me that females should be that. Only friends. So I kind of - I blurted it out. I told them everything.”

    Yelena looks at her then, open and expectant, and Kate realizes the blonde’s worried about what she might think about it. Kate’s dying to say something, anything, but her throat constricts and no matter how much she swallows it won’t seem to cooperate, so she squeezes her eyes shut and gives up the idea of speaking. Instead, she throws her arms around Yelena and brings her body close to hers, clinging to fistfuls of her sweatshirt that spill between her fingers.

    “I turned off my phone after. Because it went as terrible as you would expect and I had to hang up and they wouldn’t stop calling,” Yelena continues, her voice partly muffed by Kate’s neck. The younger girl feels the words reverberate against her skin, feels the weight of them settling in between her bones. She holds on tighter. “I spent most of the day in bed. Wanda told Nat and she came by. They dragged me out, made sure I ate something and stayed the night. But I didn’t think - I didn’t realize what it would look like, for you. I should have said something, but by the time I came to my senses it was already Sunday. That’s why I went over to your apartment, I thought it would be better to explain myself in person.”

    “But I had already left,” Kate finishes for her, and it all makes so much sense it’s almost ridiculous. All her hurt at the blonde’s radio silence evaporates and gets replaced by a newfound affection that’s almost overwhelming. Because even though she had to go through all that fucking unfair ordeal, Yelena put everything aside to make sure they were okay. She didn’t think twice to drag her sister down to New York so that she could be here with her. She didn’t try once to make excuses for herself first and waited patiently until Kate had dealt with her own thing.

    Fuck them. If they couldn’t acknowledge love, fuck them. She would.

    Wetness pools at her collarbones and brings her back to the girl in her arms. Yelena is crying without making a damn sound and somehow that’s even more gut-wrenching. “I’m sorry I scared you,” the Russian speaks again, voice hoarse, and Kate’s shaking her head before she even finishes the sentence, “No, no. There’s nothing you have to apologize for, Yel. The only thing I’m sorry for is for not having been there for you, then. But I understand. Or - fuck, I probably have no fucking idea, actually. I can’t imagine what you must have felt. What you’re feeling. But I can understand why you would forget about everything else.” And then, because she feels like maybe she should prod a little bit to assess the extent of the damage, she carefully asks, “Do you want to talk about it?”

    A warm exhale hits the skin of Kate’s neck as Yelena sighs, the blonde loosening her hold on Kate’s waist as she lifts her head and drops her arms to her sides. “There’s not much to say,” she begins resignedly, eyes firmly planted on her dangling feet, “They reacted exactly as they did when Nat came out. Anger first and guilt-tripping second. Told me how much pain I was causing them, reminded me of everything they’ve ever done for me, asked me how I could betray them like that after what my sister had put them through. Basically everything I had ever feared, only worse.” Yelena shrugs in an attempt to appear nonchalant, the tone of her voice aiming for self-deprecating sarcasm, but her effort falls flat. The hurt glimmering in her eyes betrays her.

    “I have to go up to Ohio to collect my things,” she comments after a beat of silence, while Kate’s still processing the information, and the implications of her statement render the younger girl almost speechless.

    “They kicked you out?” Kate stammers after a double-take. It’s actually not so hard to believe considering the situation, it’s just that every fact is as difficult to understand as the last. Her mind’s unable to wrap around the fact that any parent would put their kid through this simply for the fact that they don’t fit into their expectations.

    Yelena snorts then, a single tear spilling from her eye at the motion, “They can’t really kick me out when I’m already living on my own, but they did heavily imply they don’t want me around, so…”

    So no more Christmas at her parent’s. No more New Years’, no more Thanksgiving, no more Fourth of July, no more summer holidays, no more birthday parties, no more surprise visits. She has to go gather her things because they’re shutting her out.

    “God, Yel. I’m so sorry,” and it’s such a dumb thing to say, feels like so little for something so fucking huge. But there are no words to convey what she’s feeling right now, truly. Part of her feels irrationally guilty, as if she had been the one to bring this on Yelena. She knows it’s stupid, knows this would have happened whether she had stepped into the older girl’s life or not, knows Yelena has been dealing with it for longer than she has even known Kate. But still, there’s this little voice that accuses her of rushing the blonde, of having pushed her to do something she may not have been fully ready, perhaps she -

    “Kate Bishop, this is not on you,” and just like that, her mind breaks out of the endlessly spinning circle it had fallen into. Kate thinks back to a few moments before, when she had simply known with a quick glance at the other girl that something was wrong, when she had seen past her front without a single doubt. She had wondered then if she was such an open book for the older girl as Yelena was for her.

    Now she has her answer.

    Strong hands cling at her wrists, thumbs caressing her pulse point back and forth soothingly, “I wanted them to know. I was tired of hiding. I was ready, Kate. I did it for me, malyshka,” Yelena speaks ferociously, not an ounce of doubt in her voice. She gives the brunette a wet smile, her left eyebrow lifting in faux-mockery, “You shouldn’t give yourself so much credit,” she adds, and actually manages to keep a straight face for a couple of seconds. Then her smile morphs into a grin as Kate hits her shoulder and mutters a soft oh screw you while she lets her forehead fall to the blonde’s hands still holding her own.

    Their laughter dies down almost as fast as it came, and so Kate tries again, “Are you okay then?” It’s probably a silly question because she knows there’s no possible way for anyone to be okay after their parents practically disown them, but she knows Yelena will understand.

    “No, but I will be,” the older girl replies simply, her mouth slipping into the first carefree smile she’s gifted Kate since she came here. Her eyes still hold incomprehensible amounts of hurt, but there’s also hope shining underneath. There’s hope and there’s affection and there’s relief, and Kate feels her chest expand and her heart squeeze and her cheeks burn because right now it’s aimed at her. Kate’s not sure what she has ever done to deserve this but she’s damn well going to make sure she keeps it this way.

    And then, suddenly, Kate realizes that for the first time since they admitted to this unnamed thing between them, since they acknowledge its existence, there’s not a single obstacle standing in their way. There’s no third person, there are no opposing parents, there’s no reluctance on either of their sides. The thought is as exhilarating as it is vertiginous because now it’s really up to them, it can really finally happen.

    Objectively she knows perhaps today is not the best day to do this. They’re both physically bone-tired and mentally drained, and they’ve probably gone through enough emotions these past three days to last them for a lifetime. But Kate’s nothing if not impatient and she feels like she’s been waiting for this moment for forever already and she’s not about to let it slip away again. Besides, the only opinion she cares about is the one coming from the girl sitting next to her, so, “I would like to try something now, but I don’t want to presume so I need your permission first,” she whispers, bringing her forehead to rest on Yelena’s as she nudges the older girl’s nose in foreshadowing.

    Nodding swiftly, Yelena brings both her hands to rest at the nape of Kate’s neck to thread her fingers through her dark locks. “Yes,” she mumbles back, not bothering to question what Kate’s asking consent for. She’s breathing the word on the brunette’s lips, so there’s not really much to wonder about, really.

    When their lips finally meet they’re both smiling so hard it’s almost difficult to pull through the kiss. There’s wetness on Kate’s cheeks and she only realizes it’s her own tears when Yelena distances herself to kiss them away. The blonde joins their lips again forcefully once before exhaling through her nose as she rests their foreheads together, skin against skin. Their eyes meet then, and Kate knows they still have problems to solve, so many things coming their way, but as of right now they’re together, they’ve made it here. Her girl’s with her.

    “You’re staying,” Kate means to ask, states instead. Yelena only smiles, nods her head against hers, and closes her eyes.

    “Always.”

    i hope you enjoy it, i missed youuuuuu!! xxx XXIV - It's a Sin Chapter Summary It's a Sin - Elton John & Years & Years Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Kate’s never been a fan of cliches even though her life itself could be considered one - she’s not obnoxious enough to not be aware of the privilege that has surrounded her whole life, she’s perfectly aware that penthouses on the Upper East Side and trips to Europe as a gift for her good grades and private boat birthday parties at fifteen aren’t exactly the norm.

    Perhaps that’s exactly why she resents cliches so much, coming to think of it. She’s been the picture of a rich spoiled kid since she was born, and thus has worked her ass off to get rid of that label, earning her own merits through effort and stubbornness alone.

Her eyes had almost begun to water when she had forced them to remain open to stay awake in fear of dreaming about her dad, begrudgingly preferring to think about her mom consciously. And as soon as she had woken up she had felt the hit of a headache concocted with a mixture of exhaustion and anxiousness.

    wracking: what if they don’t like you, what if you don’t like them, what if they turn out to be racists, what if they end up being rude, what if they are the kind of people who yell at waiters, all that jazz.

    In this case, however, Kate already knows that the people she may encounter have a huge problem with whom she inherently is, so it kind of erases any possibility of feeling stressed about it. She’s already aware they won’t like her so she doesn’t have to try, but she sort of doesn’t like them either so it really isn’t a big deal.
